7.9   The numerical reduction
The preceding construction has replaced the end while preserving DEC and the boundary
expansion sign. A final strict conformal change puts the data in the class used for the elliptic
deformation.

Proposition 7.13 (Reduction to strict data with a controlled rest end). Let (Ω, g, K) satisfy
the exterior hypotheses of Theorem 7.2, including θ+ ≤ 0 on its smooth compact boundary, and
let m = (E 2 − |P |2 )1/2 > 0. There is a sequence of smooth exterior data (gj , Kj ) on Ω with the
following properties:

  (i) µj > |Jj |gj everywhere and θ+,j < 0 on B. The constraint densities are integrable. On
      the end their DEC margin is bounded below by cj r−3−δ for some cj > 0 and one fixed
      0 < δ < 1. For a smooth positive radius extension ρ, the constant can be decreased so that
      µj − |Jj |gj ≥ cj ρ−3−δ on all of Ω.

 (ii) Kj is compactly supported, and in a rest chart gj − δ = Ok (r−1 ) and Rgj = Ok (r−3−δ ) for
      every k. In particular Ricgj = O(r−3 ) there. Their ADM momentum is zero.

 (iii) (gj , Kj ) → (g, K) smoothly on every fixed compact subset up to the boundary, their ADM
       energies satisfy Ej → m, and agj (B) → ag (B).

Consequently it suffices to prove the energy/enclosing-area inequality for the class in (i)–(ii) in
order to prove Theorem 7.2 in its stated class.

Proof. Apply Proposition 7.10, Lemma 7.11, and Lemma 7.12 along any sequence Rj → ∞. The
resulting DEC exteriors have compactly supported second fundamental form and an exact rest
Schwarzschild end with energy tending to m. They agree with the original data on expanding
compact subsets except for a constant conformal factor tending to one. Hence they converge
smoothly on each fixed compact subset. Their boundary expansion is weakly negative.
    Apply the last part of Lemma 7.5 separately to each of these fixed exteriors, using the same
chosen 0 < δ < 1 and a compactly supported drift coefficient. Choose its positive parameter s in
Equation (7.16) small enough that the energy change, ∥ log(1 + sϕ)∥∞ , and the supremum of its
first derivative multiplied by the fixed original coordinate radius are at most 1/j. Also require
the change in the first j smooth norms on the first j members of a compact exhaustion to be at

                                                         78
most 1/j. All these quantities are finite for the fixed j-th exterior, so these choices are possible.
No estimate uniform in the receding bending radius is required for this final strictification.
    The resulting data have pointwise strict DEC and strict trapping. The quantitative end gap is
at least cj r−3−δ . On its compact complement the continuous positive function (µj − |Jj |gj )ρ3+δ
has a positive minimum. Replacing cj by the smaller of this minimum and the end constant
proves the global weighted assertion in (i). The strict conformal factor has symbol decay of all
orders and does not enlarge the support of K. On the far Schwarzschild end the drift vanishes,
so −∆ϕ = w = r−3−δ . Since the rest Schwarzschild metric has zero scalar curvature and K = 0
there, the exact u-formula gives

                                    Rgj = 8s(1 + sϕ)−5 r−3−δ .

This proves the scalar-curvature bound in (ii). The Ricci bound follows from the two-derivative
metric decay. The energy change tends to zero and the momentum change is zero. The prescribed
smallness gives smooth local convergence, proving (ii) and the local convergence and charge
assertions in (iii).
    The metrics before strictification satisfy the uniform comparisons and scaled first-derivative
bounds of Lemma 7.6, in the fixed original end chart: the reference plane, the bend, and the
radius interpolation involve only fixed linear maps and fixed radius ratios. The radial repair
preserves these bounds. The final strictification parameters were chosen to preserve them as well.
That lemma therefore gives the convergence of enclosing infima.
    This completes the preparation; no numerical inequality was used in constructing these
data.

7.11    Future timelikeness from the exterior inequality
The conformal direction already constructed allows us to move the ADM energy upward while
fixing the momentum and increasing every enclosing area. If the original ADM vector were
not future timelike, we could approach the future null cone through data to which the timelike
inequality applies. The positive area bound would persist along this family, giving a contradiction.

Theorem 7.14 (The exterior inequality without a timelike assumption). Let (Ω, g, K) satisfy all
the hypotheses of Theorem 7.2 except E > |P |δ . Then
                                                               √︄
                                       √︂                           ag (B)
                      E > |P |δ ,           E 2 − |P |2δ ≥                 ,            B = ∂Ω.
                                                                     16π

No extension of the data across B is required.

Proof. Lemma 7.5 supplies a positive function ϕ, a constant Aϕ > 0, and, for every finite s ≥ 0,
the data
                           gs = (1 + sϕ)4 g,    Ks = (1 + sϕ)2 K.
These data retain completeness, integrable constraint densities, DEC, weak future trapping of
B, and the required differentiated decay. The lemma makes no sign assumption on the original
ADM vector. Its charge and area identities, Equations (7.17) and (7.18), give

                      Es = E + sAϕ ,             Ps = P,            ags (B) ≥ ag (B) > 0,

where the last strict inequality is Lemma 7.3.
   Suppose E ≤ |P |δ , and set s0 = (|P |δ − E)/Aϕ . This is a finite nonnegative number. For
every s > s0 the deformed vector is future timelike, so Theorem 7.2, applied to these fixed data,
implies                                            √︄            √︄

As s ↓ s0 the left side tends to zero, while the final lower bound is fixed and positive. This
contradiction proves E > |P |δ . The timelike exterior inequality now applies directly to (Ω, g, K)
and gives the stated bound.




                                                       80
7.12    Complete data with finitely many ends
We now pass from a one-ended exterior to a complete boundaryless initial-data manifold. The
ambient enclosing class allows other ends to remain on the chosen side. We first isolate the chosen
end to use the exterior theorem, and then compare the two area infima in the direction required
for the conclusion.

Definition 7.15 (Admissible boundary and ambient enclosing area). Let (M, g, K) be a smooth
three-dimensional initial data set without boundary, with M connected, and fix one asymptotically
flat end e. An admissible boundary for e is a nonempty compact smooth embedded two-sided
surface S = ∂D+ , where D+ is a smooth open region containing the entire sufficiently distant
part of e. The surface and the region may be disconnected, and D+ may contain other ends. For
the normal ν pointing into D+ , require

                                           HS + trS K ≤ 0.

Its minimum enclosing area is

The full boundary area is counted, including portions coincident with S. Competitor regions D′+
may be disconnected and may retain other ends. Their boundaries need be neither trapped nor
minimal. The definition imposes no sign condition on the inward null expansion.

Theorem 7.16 (Spacetime Penrose inequality for complete initial data). Let (M, g, K) be a
connected orientable smooth three-dimensional initial data set without boundary, with g complete.
Suppose that outside a compact set M has finitely many asymptotically flat ends. On each end,
in its asymptotic coordinates, assume

                        g − δ = O2 (r−q ),        K = O1 (r−1−q ),     q > 12 ,

and finite ADM energy and momentum limits with the normalizations (7.4)–(7.5). The decay
exponents may differ between ends. Assume that the physical constraint densities (7.1) obey
µ ≥ |J|g and that both µ and |J|g are integrable on M .
    For every chosen end and every admissible boundary S in Definition 7.15, its ADM vector
(E, P ) satisfies                                       √︄
                                             √︂                 Amin (S)
                             E > |P |δ ,       E 2 − |P |2δ ≥            .                     (7.37)
                                                                 16π
Proof. Let D0 be the connected component of D+ containing the distant chosen end. Its boundary
is a union of components of S. It is nonempty: otherwise D0 would be both open and closed in
the connected manifold M , and hence equal to M , contradicting S ̸= ∅. The closure X0 = D0
is therefore a smooth manifold with nonempty compact boundary. Since S is compact, each
sufficiently distant end of M lies either entirely in D0 or entirely outside it. Thus X0 has finitely
many ends, precisely those original end tails contained in D0 .
    For every end of X0 other than the chosen one, remove a sufficiently distant open coordinate
tail. The tails can be chosen disjoint and disjoint from S. Denote the remainder by Ω. It is
connected: a path segment entering one of the removed tails can be replaced by a path on the

                                                   81
connected coordinate sphere bounding that tail. Consequently Ω is a one-ended smooth exterior
with compact boundary                             ⋃︂
                               B = ∂D0 ∪                 SRi .
                                                     other ends of X0

This boundary is nonempty because ∂D0 is nonempty.
   The set Ω is closed in the complete manifold (M, g), and it is complete for its intrinsic distance
with B included. Indeed, an intrinsic Cauchy sequence is ambient Cauchy and has its ambient
limit in Ω. At an interior limit point the two distances are locally comparable. At a boundary
limit point, a smooth boundary chart identifies Ω locally with a half-ball and gives the same
comparison, so the sequence converges intrinsically as well. Restricting the data preserves DEC
and integrability. The chosen end is unchanged, so its decay and ADM vector remain the original
ones.
   On ∂D0 the normal into Ω is the normal into D+ , and the future expansion is nonpositive.
On an added sphere SRi , the normal into Ω points toward decreasing radius in the removed end.
The differentiated asymptotic assumptions give
                                 2
                      HSRi = −      + O(Ri−1−qi ),           trSRi K = O(Ri−1−qi ).
                                 Ri
Choose each Ri large enough that their sum is negative. Every component of B is then weakly
future outer trapped for the normal into Ω. Theorem 7.14 applies and yields
                                                                      √︄
                                                √︂                         ag (B)
                              E > |P |δ ,            E 2 − |P |2δ ≥               .           (7.38)
                                                                            16π
    It remains to compare the enclosing classes. We claim

                                            ag (B) ≥ Amin (S).                                (7.39)

Take first a smooth enclosing cut Γ contained in the interior of Ω. The connected component on
its distant chosen-end side is a smooth open region D′+ ⊂ D0 ⊂ D+ . Its boundary consists of
components of Γ, so it is compact and smooth and has area at most Areag (Γ). It is therefore
an admissible competitor in (7.36). For a cut that meets or coincides with B, a smooth collar
push into the interior, with the obstacle side filled, gives enclosing cuts whose areas converge
to its full area. This applies also to contact with the added coordinate spheres. Hence every
full enclosing cut of Ω has area at least Amin (S), proving (7.39) after taking the infimum. The
discarded components of D+ do not obstruct this comparison: the ambient definition permits the
chosen-end component alone as a competitor. Combining the area comparison with (7.38) proves
the theorem.

    The auxiliary exterior excludes the other distant ends, whereas the ambient competitor class
allows them. The proof uses only the one-sided comparison (7.39). Rigidity is a separate question
about the original data; it requires its own argument rather than equality transport through these
conformal changes or end truncations.


8    Weak asymptotics in four dimensions
We prove a lower bound for the ADM rest mass in terms of the least three-volume needed to
separate a chosen asymptotically flat end from the compact weakly trapped boundary and the

                                                      82
other ends. The task is to bring two derivatives of the metric and one derivative of the second
fundamental form within the scope of Theorem 2.3. We first replace the selected end by a rest
end while controlling every cut, then make the dominant energy and boundary inequalities strict.
The prepared theorem is applied to each fixed resulting datum; only its numerical conclusion is
passed to the limit.

8.1   Initial data and enclosing cuts
Four is the spatial dimension; the associated spacetime dimension is five, and a hypersurface
area is a three-dimensional Riemannian volume. Here ω3 = |S 3 | = 2π 2 , and we write τ = trg K.
Specializing the constraint convention already fixed in the paper gives

                   2µ = Rg + (trg K)2 − |K|2g ,         Ji = ∇j (Kij − (trg K)gij ).

Here Rg = Scalg denotes scalar curvature and ∇ is the Levi–Civita connection of g. The
cosmological constant is zero. We use ∆g = divg gradg . For a hypersurface with specified unit
normal ν, write

              H = divtan ν,      trtan K = (g ij − ν i ν j )Kij ,   ΘK = H + trtan K.

At an inner boundary the normal points into the exterior. In an auxiliary trapped-region
construction it points out of the bounded region.
   The notation Oj (r−a ) includes coordinate derivatives of order k ≤ j, bounded by O(r−a−k ).
On an asymptotically flat end the ADM normalization is
                                 1
                                            ∫︂
                           E=        lim      (∂j gij − ∂i gjj )niδ dAδ ,                    (8.1)
                                6ω3 R→∞ |x|=R

Here nδ and dAδ are Euclidean. Whenever a charge is used below, its stated limit is required to
exist.

Definition 8.1 (Enclosing cuts). Let M have nonempty compact boundary S and finitely many
asymptotically flat ends, and fix an end e. An admissible outer domain De is a connected smooth
codimension-zero submanifold with boundary, closed as a subset of M , such that its manifold
interior lies in int M , it contains the entire sufficiently distant part of e, and it contains no
sufficiently distant part of any other end. Its entire intrinsic manifold boundary Γ = ∂man De is
required to be compact. The enclosing volume is

                                      Ae (S) = inf |∂man De |g .
                                                  De

All frontier coincident with S is counted. The cut may be disconnected. In the one-ended case
De = M is allowed and has intrinsic boundary S. We then also write A∗ for this infimum.

    Only these smooth cuts are used. Their infimum need not be attained. The connected outer
domain excludes the distant parts of all other ends, although its cut may be disconnected. Thus
all other ends and the entire original boundary are obstacles to the selected outer domain. This
end-isolating convention remains distinct from the three-dimensional complete-data class, whose
chosen-end region may be disconnected and may retain other ends.

8.2   The exact cut class and removal of other ends
Lemma 8.3 (Cut reduction and positivity). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 8.2, the quantity
Ae (S) is finite and strictly positive for every end e. Truncating all other ends at sufficiently
large coordinate spheres gives a connected, complete one-ended exterior MT with compact smooth
boundary ST . All its boundary components have future nonpositive expansion with the normal
into MT . Its selected-end charges are the original charges, and

                        A∗ (MT , g) ≥ Ae (S),                lim A∗ (MT , g) = Ae (S).             (8.5)
                                                            T →∞

The common parameter T in the limit can be replaced by any exhaustion in which every unwanted
truncation radius tends to infinity.


                                                           84
Proof. A sufficiently distant sphere in the chosen end bounds an admissible outer domain, so the
infimum is finite. To obtain a positive lower bound, choose an embedded arc from an open disk in
S to the chosen end, and continue it along a straight coordinate ray. The compact part of the arc
can be chosen embedded and disjoint from the boundary except at its initial point. A boundary
collar and the tubular neighborhood theorem give a proper tube with coordinates
                                          [0, ∞) × B 3 −→ M.
Its initial section lies in S; its far part is a Euclidean tube of fixed transverse coordinate size.
Choose a smooth nonnegative function η compactly supported in B 3 , with integral one, and
define in this tube
                                       α = η(z) dz 1 ∧ dz 2 ∧ dz 3 .
Extend α by zero across the lateral tube boundary. It is a smooth closed three-form on M .
Its comass, meaning the supremum of its absolute value on unit simple three-vectors, has a
finite bound Cα : the initial part is compact, and the far metric is uniformly comparable with
the Euclidean metric. A sufficiently distant selected-end sphere has α-integral one, after fixing
orientation.
    Let De be any admissible outer domain with cut Γ. Cut off its selected-end tail at a sphere
beyond Γ. The resulting domain is compact, since De contains no distant part of another end. Its
boundary is the distant sphere together with the entire intrinsic boundary Γ, with the boundary
orientations. Stokes’ theorem gives
                                    ⃓∫︂ ⃓
                                                      |Γ|g ≥ Cα−1 .
                                    ⃓   ⃓
                                    ⃓ α⃓ = 1,
                                    ⃓   ⃓
                                      Γ

This proof applies to disconnected cuts and to frontier on S. In particular, it proves positivity
before any end is truncated.
   On an unwanted end, the normal of the truncation sphere that points into the retained
manifold is the inward radial normal. The given decay therefore gives
                        H = −3T −1 + O(T −1−q ),         trST K = O(T −1−q ).
The new boundary has strictly negative future expansion for large T . The other boundary
components are the original ones. Removing the open tails leaves a connected manifold: a path
entering a removed tail can have that portion replaced by a path along its connected spherical
cross-section. The retained manifold is closed in M . It is complete for its intrinsic metric as well.
Indeed, an intrinsic Cauchy sequence is ambient Cauchy and has a limit in the retained set; near
a smooth boundary or an interior point, the intrinsic and ambient local distances are comparable
in a half-ball or ball chart.
    Every admissible outer domain in MT , viewed as a submanifold of M , is an admissible outer
domain for e. A part of its frontier on a truncation sphere becomes a hypersurface in the interior of
M , and remains part of its intrinsic boundary. This proves the first inequality in (8.5). Conversely,
a fixed original admissible domain has compact frontier and contains no tail of any unwanted
end. It is consequently contained in MT for all sufficiently large truncation radii and is then
an admissible domain there. Applying this observation to cuts with area within any prescribed
positive error of Ae (S) proves the limit. The selected-end data have not been changed, so neither
selected-end charge changes.

   We may thus work on one-ended data until the final transfer. The boundary is always included.
No finite-perimeter relaxation or minimizing cut is needed in this section.

Lemma 8.4 (Conformal constraint and boundary formulas). Let u > 0 be smooth, and set
gu = u2 g, Ku = uK. In dimension four, as an identity of scalar functions and an identity of

                                        u2 µu = µ − 3u−1 ∆g u,                                    (8.6)

Proof. Use Equation (3.2) with n = 4, k = 3, p = 2, and s = log u. The constraint normalization
is unchanged. At zero vector argument its first identity gives u2 µu = µ − 3∆ log u − 3|d log u|2 =
µ − 3u−1 ∆u. The covector formula proved with that identity gives uJu = J + 3K(∇ log u, ·);
taking its gu norm adds another factor u−1 . The triangle inequality then gives Equation (8.8),
equivalently the U = u specialization of Equation (3.3). The boundary formula reads ΘKu =
u−1 (ΘK + 3∂ν log u), with the same chosen normal. The identities require smooth fields and
u > 0 only, so this application does not strengthen the original O2 /O1 decay hypotheses.

8.4   Matching the charges before making a rest end
                                         √︁
A rest end must have energy close to E 2 − |P |2 , not to E. We therefore first attach a vacuum
reference plane carrying exactly (E, P ), and only then bend that plane to a static slice. Compact
linear corrections make the interpolation deficit small; a positive conformal repair restores DEC
at vanishing mass cost.

The reference plane
Lemma 8.6 (Explicit reference-plane charges). For any E > |P |, put m = (E 2 − |P |2 )1/2 . A
spacelike plane in the Schwarzschild–Tangherlini spacetime of mass m, sufficiently far from its
central region, has induced vacuum data (g∗ , K∗ ) with charges exactly (E, P ), and

                 g∗ − δ = Ok (r−2 ),       K∗ = Ok (r−3 )        for every integer k ≥ 0.

Here K∗ is one half the metric rate in the future normal direction.

Proof. Use Lemma 3.2 with n = 4, k = 3, p = 2, and M = m. Its exact isotropic spacetime
metric is                                 )︄2
                        (︄                                      )︃ 4
                           1 − m/(2|y|2 )                   m 2 ∑︂
                                                    (︃
                 g=−                          db2
                                                  +    1 +           (dy i )2 .   (8.10)
                           1 + m/(2|y|2 )                  2|y|2 i=1

Rotate the first axis into the direction of P and put v = |P |/E, γ = (1 − v 2 )−1/2 . The Lorentz
plane in that lemma has E∗ = mγ = E and P∗1 = mγv = |P |, with all transverse momenta
zero. These are the present factors 1/(6ω3 ) and 1/(3ω3 ): in particular no mass is rescaled. The
leading energy flux is 6mγ 2 xi ρ−4 , the leading momentum tensor is the k = 3 instance of (3.7),
and the angular integral in (3.8) is ω3 /γ. Thus the sign and normalization are the same ones used
here. The second form uses the same future-normal convention. Finally the metric and tensor
remainders in that lemma are Od (r−4 ) and Od (r−5 ), with leading orders r−2 and r−3 , through
every fixed derivative order. Gauss–Codazzi in the vacuum metric (8.10) proves that the reference
data are vacuum.

Compact inverses for the linear constraints
Localized constraint deformation and finite-dimensional adjoint obstructions have a substantial
history [14, 13]. The compact operators below are used to reduce, rather than silently solve away,
the interpolation defect. The subsequent DEC repair and comparison of every admissible cut are
separate conclusions of this section.
    On Euclidean four-space write

                   Lb = ∂i ∂j (bij − (trδ b)δij ),        (Dk)i = ∂j (kij − (trδ k)δij ).   (8.11)

These are the linearizations at (δ, 0) of 2µ and J. The next lemma identifies every compatibility
condition needed for a compact correction.




                                                     87
Lemma 8.7 (Compact linear constraint corrections). Fix a compact subannulus U0 of a bounded
connected Euclidean annulus U . If f and F are smooth and supported in U0 , and
                             ∫︂
                                  f ϕ dz = 0 for every affine ϕ,
                        ∫︂                                                                   (8.12)
                             Fi Y i dz = 0 for every Euclidean Killing field Y,

there are symmetric smooth tensors b, k, supported in a fixed compact subset of U , with Lb = f ,
Dk = F . For every integer j ≥ 0 and every 1 < p1 < ∞, they can be chosen linearly in the
sources so that
                    ∥b∥W j+2,p1 ≤ C∥f ∥W j,p1 ,  ∥k∥W j+1,p1 ≤ C∥F ∥W j,p1 .              (8.13)
The constant depends only on U0 , U, j, p1 and fixed localization choices. The moment conditions
are also necessary for compactly supported solutions.
Proof. Lemma 3.3 applies with n = 4 on the given connected annulus. Its scalar operator S is L,
and its vector operator D is D. Its affine and Euclidean Killing moments are exactly (8.12). The
inverse trace reversal specializes to

                                              b = S − 13 (trδ S)δ,                           (8.14)

so b − (trδ b)δ = S. The scalar construction gains two Sobolev derivatives. The symmetric
divergence construction first gains one derivative, uses a further divergence inverse to correct the
skew part, and differentiates that correction once; its net gain is one. This proves (8.13), with
support in a fixed larger compact subset and linear dependence on the sources. No quantitative
derivative bound for the initial data is being inferred here; the commutator calculation below
establishes the needed source norms from O2 /O1 .
    For necessity, integration by parts pairs Lb with Hess ϕ − (∆ϕ)δ, which vanishes for affine ϕ,
and pairs Dk with the symmetric gradient of a Killing field after trace reversal. The latter also
vanishes. Thus all and only the stated moments obstruct these compact inverses.

8.5    Rest-end replacement and comparison of all cuts
Proposition 8.8 (Rest-end replacement). Let (M, g, K) satisfy the one-ended hypotheses of
Theorem 8.2, and suppose E > |P |. Put m = (E 2 − |P |2 )1/2 . There is a sequence of smooth
data (gR , KR ) on the same manifold, complete with the same compact boundary, satisfying the
dominant energy condition and the same future boundary inequality, such that:
  (i) KR is compactly supported, and the ultimate end is an exact static Schwarzschild–Tangherlini
      spatial end;

 (ii) its energy is mR = m + o(1), its momentum is zero, and both constraint densities are
      integrable;

 (iii) A∗ (gR ) ≥ (1 − o(1))A∗ (g).
Every error here tends to zero as R → ∞ for the fixed original data and fixed timelike vector. No
uniformity as E − |P | tends to zero is asserted or needed.
Proof. Use Lemma 8.6 for a reference end with exactly the original charges. We first join the
data to this plane on R < r < 4R, controlling the full pointwise constraint deficit.

                                                      88
The scaled commutators. Fix 1 < p < min(2, q), write x = Rz, and on 1 < |z| < 4 use the
scaled component fields
                          g(Rz),     k(z) = RK(Rz),
and their reference counterparts. This corresponds to rescaling lengths by R−1 , so both constraint
densities scale by R2 . The metric deviations and tensors have size O(R−p ) in C 2 and C 1 ,
respectively. Expanding the constraint map about (δ, 0) gives its linear part (L, D) from (8.11),
with remainder bounded in C 0 by CR−2p . This follows directly from the scalar curvature formula:
the nonlinear metric terms are bounded by C(|b||D2 b|+|Db|2 ); the other scalar terms are quadratic
in k. The momentum remainder is bounded by C(|b||Dk| + |Db||k|).
    Choose a fixed smooth radial ψ, equal to one near |z| = 1 and zero near |z| = 4, with 0 ≤ ψ ≤ 1.
Blend the metric components and tensors by ψ. If b is the difference of the two scaled metric
perturbations and k the difference of the two scaled tensors, the errors of the linear constraints
relative to the blended sources are fR = [L, ψ]b and FR = [D, ψ]k. For Tij = bij − (trδ b)δij ,
                                   [L, ψ]b = (∂i ∂j ψ)Tij + 2(∂i ψ)∂j Tij ,                        (8.15)
                                ([D, ψ]k)i = (∂j ψ)(kij − (trδ k)δij ).                            (8.16)
These expressions are supported in a fixed compact subannulus, and their C 1 norms are O(R−p ).
In particular, the first bound uses only two metric derivatives, and the second only one tensor
derivative.

All compatibility moments are small. We prove the sharper bound o(R−2 ) for every moment
in (8.12). Work momentarily in the physical coordinates, with
            h = g − g∗ ,   T = K − (trδ K)δ − K∗ + (trδ K∗ )δ,         f = Lh,    Fi = ∂j Tij .
Both f and F belong to L1 on the end. Indeed, the difference between the physical constraints
and their Euclidean linear parts is O(r−2−2 min(q,2) ), which is integrable in dimension four. The
physical densities are integrable by hypothesis and by reference vacuum, and Euclidean and
physical measures and norms are uniformly comparable there.
   An elementary consequence of integrability is
                                   ∫︂
                                               r(|f | + |F |) dx = o(R).                           (8.17)
                                    r0 <r<4R

To see this, split at a fixed radius L. The integral up to L, divided by R, tends to zero. The
remaining integral divided by R is at most four times the L1 tail beyond L, which can be made
arbitrarily small. A finite first moment is not required.
   For an affine function ϕ, define the flux primitive
                           Qϕ,i = ϕ(∂j hij − ∂i hjj ) − (∂j ϕ)hij + (∂i ϕ)hjj .
Its divergence is ϕf . For a Killing field Y , symmetry of T gives ∂j (Y i Tij ) = Y i Fi . Let Fϕ (r) and
GY (r) be the corresponding outward Euclidean sphere fluxes. For constant tests, matching the
ADM charges gives
                                   F1 (r) = o(1),    Gei (r) = o(1).
In the momentum flux the replacement of trg K g by (trδ K)δ costs at most O(r2−2 min(q,2) ) = o(1).
For homogeneous linear scalar tests and rotational fields, integration from a fixed sphere and
(8.17) instead give
                               Fϕ (r) = o(r),    GY (r) = o(r).

8.6       Strict trapping and a positive energy margin
The rest-end replacement preserves weak trapping and the dominant energy condition. The
prepared construction requires strict inequalities. We now obtain them by a conformal change
whose size tends uniformly to zero and whose change in ADM energy can be computed exactly.
All constants in this step belong to one fixed rest-end metric. In particular, when that metric
depends on the replacement radius R, we keep R fixed throughout this construction.
    The drift-Poisson equation used below is related to Jaracz’s conformal strictification [28]. The
proof here supplies the four-dimensional equation, its nonzero inner Neumann condition, and the
charge and full-cut estimates needed for the numerical argument.
Lemma 8.9 (Strictification of a fixed rest end). Let (M, g, K) be a smooth connected four-
dimensional exterior, complete with its nonempty compact smooth boundary included, with one
Euclidean coordinate end and compact complement. Suppose µ ≥ |J|g and ΘK ≤ 0 on every
boundary component, where the boundary normal ν points into M . Assume that K has compact
support and that, outside a compact set,

8.7   Applying the prepared theorem and returning to the original end
The proper radial comparison protects the original enclosing area during rest-end replacement.
Strictification then changes the cut infimum by a factor tending to one at each fixed replacement
radius. We apply the prepared inequality after both constructions, remove strictification at that
fixed radius, and finally send the replacement radius to infinity.
Proposition 8.10 (Transfer from prepared data). Theorem 2.3, applied in dimension four,
implies Theorem 8.2.
Proof. First let the original exterior have one end and E > |P |. Set m = (E 2 − |P |2 )1/2 > 0.
Proposition 8.8 gives data (gR , KR ) with zero momentum and energy mR → m. Its full-cut
comparison is
                           A∗ (gR ) ≥ (1 + ϵR )−3/2 A∗ (g), ϵR −→ 0.                      (8.38)
Fix one sufficiently large replacement radius R. For any fixed 0 < δ1 < 1, Lemma 8.9 makes
(gR,s , KR,s ) a prepared exterior for all sufficiently small s > 0. Indeed it is the same smooth
connected orientable one-ended exterior, complete with its compact boundary included and
with compact complement of its coordinate end. It has finite ADM energy and a compactly
supported tensor, so its ADM momentum is zero. On the end, gR,s − δ = Od (r−2 ) for every fixed
derivative order d, its scalar curvature is O(r−4−δ1 ), and its constraint densities are integrable.
The boundary is strictly future trapped, and the global margin is
                           µR,s − |JR,s |gR,s ≥ cR,s ρ−4−δ1 ,                 cR,s > 0,
where ρ is a positive smooth extension of the rest-end radius. These are the geometric and decay
conditions of Definition 2.1, with n = 4 and β = δ1 .
    The area condition follows along the same comparisons. In one end, Definition 8.1 is exactly
the prepared full-cut class: the outer domain is connected and closed, its interior lies in the
open exterior, and its entire compact intrinsic boundary is counted, including every component
and all coincidence with the original boundary. Lemma 8.3 gives A∗ (g) > 0; Equation (8.38)
and strictification give A∗ (gR,s ) ≥ A∗ (gR ) > 0. The constraint and ADM normalizations also
agree: 2µ = R + (tr K)2 − |K|2 , J = div(K − (tr K)g), and the energy and momentum factors
are 1/(6ω3 ) and 1/(3ω3 ). No rescaling is made.

uniform in R or s.

Lemma 8.5 gives, separately for each j, data of energy |P | + aj , momentum P , and enclosing

                                                                                         )︃2/3

                            √︂                                             (︃
                                (|P | + aj )2 − |P |2 ≥                                           > 0.

The right side is fixed and positive by Lemma 8.3, while the left side tends to zero. This
contradiction includes zero, negative, and null energy cases. No preparation geometry is passed
to a limit as the boost degenerates: the timelike numerical result was applied afresh to each shell
datum. Thus E > |P | and (8.40) hold for every one-ended original datum in the theorem.
    Finally let the original manifold have finitely many ends and fix one end e. Truncate every
other end at a sufficiently large sphere as in Lemma 8.3. The retained manifold is a complete
connected one-ended exterior. Its added boundary components are strictly future trapped, its
selected-end charges are still (Ee , Pe ), and its full-cut infimum is at least Ae (S). Applying the
one-ended result gives                    √︄

Positivity of Ae (S) was proved before any truncation. This gives both the asserted inequality
and strict future timelikeness at e.

   The one-ended cases of Theorems 7.14 and 8.2 together prove Theorem 1.5.

8.8   The general maximal DEC energy inequality
The maximal special case requires no assumption that a momentum limit exists. The following
end calculation supplies that limit from the decay and integrability already imposed on the data.

Lemma 8.11 (Existence of the momentum flux). On a smooth four-dimensional asymptotically
flat end, suppose g − δ = O2 (r−q ) and K = O1 (r−1−q ) for some q > 1. If |J|g is integrable, where
Ji = ∇j (Kij − (trg K)gij ), then each ADM momentum limit in (8.2) exists and is finite.

Proof. In the given coordinates set
                         g                                              δ
                        πij = Kij − (trg K)gij ,                       πij = Kij − (trδ K)δij .

bound in (8.41). Each connection term has size O(r−1−q )O(r−1−q ). These are exactly the asserted
errors.

metric and Euclidean volume and covector norms are uniformly comparable, so ∂j πij      δ ∈ L1 . The

function of R, with finite limit. By the first bound in (8.41), its difference from the flux of π g is

Corollary 8.12 (Maximal DEC energy inequality). Let (X 4 , gX , KX ) be smooth and connected,
with X orientable, gX complete as a metric space with its nonempty compact smooth boundary
SX included, and with exactly one end, which is asymptotically flat and diffeomorphic to the
complement of a closed ball in R4 . Let KX be a smooth symmetric covariant two-tensor. Suppose

                   trgX KX = 0,       µX = 12 (RgX − |KX |2gX ) ≥ | divgX KX |gX ,
                                µX , | divgX KX |gX ∈ L1 (X, dVgX ).

Assume for some q > 1 that

                             gX − δ = O2 (r−q ),         KX = O1 (r−1−q ),

and that the ADM energy EX in (8.1) is finite. On every component of SX , require H +trSX KX ≤
0 with the normal into X. For the one-ended enclosing-cut infimum of Definition 8.1, denoted
here by agX (SX ), one has

The boundary may have any topology and finitely many components. No symmetry, vacuum, spin,
outermostness, outer area-minimization or momentum-flux assumption is imposed.
Proof. Maximality identifies the momentum density with JX = divgX KX . Lemma 8.11 supplies
the finite ADM momentum. All hypotheses of Theorem 8.2 therefore hold for this one-ended
exterior. Its invariant-mass inequality in particular gives the displayed energy bound. The proof
of Theorem 8.2 uses Theorem 2.3, independently of this corollary. This numerical consequence
retains its broad maximal-DEC scope; the separate direct maximal-vacuum construction has its
own hypotheses.
